Late-time asymptotics for geometric wave equations with inverse-square potentials
Dejan Gajic

Abstract
We introduce a new, physical-space-based method for deriving the precise leading-order late-time behaviour of solutions to geometric wave equations on asymptotically flat spacetime backgrounds and apply it to the setting of wave equations with asymptotically inverse-square potentials on Schwarzschild black holes. This provides a useful toy model setting for introducing methods that are applicable to more general linear and nonlinear geometric wave equations, such as wave equations for electromagnetically charged scalar fields, wave equations on extremal Kerr black holes and geometric wave equations in even space dimensions, where existing proofs for deriving precise late-time asymptotics might not apply. The method we introduce relies on exploiting the spatial decay properties of time integrals of solutions to derive the existence and precise genericity properties of asymptotic late-time tails and obtain sharp, uniform decay estimates in time.



1
Introduction

The leading-order late-time behaviour of solutions to geometric wave equations of the form:

\square_{g_{M}}\phi=0,

(1.1)

with
\square_{g_{M}}
denoting the geometric wave operator with respect to the Schwarzschild spacetime metric with mass
M
:

g_{M}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)dt^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}),

(1.2)

is dictated by inverse-polynomially decaying tails with coefficients and rates that are connected to the existence of conserved charges
at infinity
.
1
1

1

More precisely, the conserved charges are quantities that are evaluated at future null infinity and they are linear in the radiation field of
\phi
and appropriate rescaled derivatives; see §
1.1
.
These conserved charges are generalizations of the
Newman–Penrose constants
introduced in
[
NP65
,
NP68
]
. In
[
AAG18b
,
AAG18a
,
AAG21b
]
, a physical-space based mechanism was developed for exploiting these conserved charges to obtain precise leading-order late-time asymptotics and demonstrate the existence of inverse-polynomially decaying tails (the corresponding decay rates are referred to as “Price’s law”, as they were first suggested in
[
Pri72
]
). This mechanism has been shown to be robust under the replacement of
g_{M}
with more general asymptotically flat spacetimes, like the Kerr black hole spacetimes
[
AAG21a
]
and more general equations, like the Teukolsky equations of linearized gravity
[
MZ21a
,
MZ21b
]
.

Besides elucidating the late-time properties of gravitational radiation at infinity in the setting of the Einstein equations of general relativity, understanding the existence and decay properties of late-time tails is necessary for determining the singularity properties in dynamical black hole interiors and addressing the
strong cosmic censorship conjecture
in general relativity; see for example
[
LS16
,
LO19a
,
LO19b
,
Sbi22
]
and references therein.

The mechanism described above, which relies on the existence of conserved charges at infinity, still applies when we consider geometric wave equations with a potential
V
,

\square_{g_{M}}\phi=V\phi,

(1.3)

where
V
denotes a time-independent function satisfying
|V|\lesssim r^{-p}
for
p>2
with respect to a suitable radial coordinate
r
.

In the present paper, we consider instead potentials with
p=2
:

\displaystyle\square_{g_{M}}\phi=

\displaystyle\>V_{\alpha}\phi,

(1.4)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}(r)\sim

\displaystyle\>\alpha r^{-2}\hskip 10.00002pt(r\to\infty),

(1.5)

with the additional global assumption that
V_{\alpha}(r)\geq-\frac{1}{4}r^{-2}
,
2
2

2

In fact, the methods of the present paper can be extended to a larger class of potentials
V
with
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
that do not need to satisfy
V(r)\geq-\frac{1}{4}r^{-2}
globally; see assumption
A’)
in §
3.1
for more details.
which requires in particular the restriction to
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
. Potentials satisfying (
1.5
) are
asymptotically scale-critical
.
3
3

3

Here, scale-critical means that the potential has the property that for
\phi
solutions to (
1.3
) the rescaled function
\phi_{\lambda}(t,r,\theta,\varphi)~=\lambda^{-2}\phi(\lambda t,\lambda r,\theta,\varphi)
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, is also a solution to (
1.3
). The potential
V(r)=\alpha r^{-2}
would therefore be exactly scale-critical. Inverse-square asymptotics for
V
can also be thought of as “critical” in the following sense: when
\alpha<0
, the operator
\Delta-V
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has an infinite discrete spectrum if
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-p}
with
p<2
, whereas the discrete spectrum is finite if
p=2
and
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
or if
p>2
; see Theorem XIII.6 of
[
RS78
]
.
We will moreover assume that
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(the case where
\alpha=l(l+1)
is discussed in §
1.1
).

For wave equations with asymptotically inverse-square potentials
V_{\alpha}
, the mechanism relying on the existence of conserved charges along future null infinity
breaks down
.
The aim of the present paper is to develop a new physical-space based mechanism for deriving late-time asymptotics in a general setting of geometric wave equations on asymptotically flat spacetimes with inverse-square potentials that avoids the use of conserved charges at infinity.

The study of late-time asymptotics for (
1.4
) is motivated by the wave equation for electromagnetically charged scalar fields (on black hole spacetimes), as well as the wave equation on extremal Kerr black hole spacetimes and wave equations on asymptotically flat spacetimes in even space dimensions. In all of these settings, we encounter analogous difficulties to those faced in the study of (
1.4
) when trying to derive the leading-order term in the late-time asymptotics of solutions to the corresponding equations. This motivates the need for the development of sufficiently robust mathematical tools, which are introduced in the present paper; see §
1.2
for more details.

To simplify the exposition, we will restrict the consideration in the present paper to Schwarzschild spacetime backgrounds, but the main methods and philosophy apply also when considering more general asymptotically flat spacetimes. The proofs in the paper will moreover be entirely self-contained. We state now the first version of the main result:

Theorem 1.1

(First version)
.

Let
\phi
be a solution to (
1.4
) arising from suitably regular and decaying initial data. Then:

(i)

(Late-time asymptotics)
There exist constants
C=C(V_{\alpha},M)>0
and
\nu=\nu(\alpha)>0
such that for a suitable hyperboloidal
4
4

4

The level sets of
\tau
intersect the event horizon to the future of the bifurcation sphere and are asymptotically hyperboloidal, i.e. they can be thought of as intersecting future null infinity appropriately.
time coordinate
\tau\geq 0
, a radial coordinate
r
and spherical coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
, we can estimate:

\displaystyle\left|\phi(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)-Q_{\alpha}[\phi]w_{0}(r)f(\tau,r)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\>CD[\phi]\cdot r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}\cdot f(\tau,r)\cdot(\tau+M)^{-\nu},\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}

\displaystyle f(\tau,r)=

\displaystyle\>(\tau+2r)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}(\tau+M)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}},

(1.6)

\displaystyle\beta_{0}=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{1+4\alpha},

with
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
a constant that can be explicitly determined from initial data,
w_{0}
denoting the stationary, spherically symmetric solution to (
1.4
) that has the asymptotic behaviour
w_{0}(r)\sim r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}
as
r\to\infty
and
D[\phi]
an initial data norm.

(ii)

(Genericity of late-time tails)
The constant
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
is non-vanishing for an open and dense subset
\mathcal{G}
of initial data, with respect to an appropriate weighted
L^{1}
-type initial data norm, even when restricting to compactly supported initial data, and the complement
\mathcal{G}^{c}
has codimension 1.

We refer to Theorem
4.1
for a more precise version of the above theorem, including precise expressions for the initial data quantities
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
and
D[\phi]
, as well as the exponent
\nu
, and moreover, refined late-time asymptotics when projecting
\phi
to spherical harmonics of degree
\geq\ell
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem
1.1
implies in particular that solutions to (
1.4
) have late-time asymptotics in terms of standard Schwarzschild coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
with rates that depend on the coefficient
\alpha
in the following way: for
r_{0}\geq 2M

\displaystyle\phi|_{\{r=r_{0}\}}\sim

\displaystyle\>(t+r_{*}(r_{0}))^{-(1+\sqrt{1+4\alpha})}\hskip 10.00002pt(t+r_{*}(r_{0})\to\infty),

\displaystyle r\phi|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}=\{t+r_{*}=\infty,t-r_{*}<\infty\}}\sim

\displaystyle\>(t-r_{*})^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{1+4\alpha})}\hskip 10.00002pt(t-r_{*}\to\infty),

with
t
the standard Schwarzschild time coordinate and
r_{*}(r)
the tortoise coordinate on Schwarzschild, which satisfies
\frac{dr_{*}}{dr}=(1-\frac{2M}{r})^{-1}
. The quantities
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
can
a posteriori
be interpreted as conserved charges at infinity for time integrals of
\phi
; see Remark
4.1
.

1.1
A comparison with more rapidly decaying potentials and Price’s law for late-time tails

It was first shown in
[
AAG18a
,
AAG18b
]
that in the setting of (
1.1
) (i.e. the wave equation on Schwarzschild without a potential), (
(i)
) holds for conformally regular initial data, with
\alpha=0
(in which case
w_{0}\equiv 1
) and
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]=4I_{0}[\phi]
, where
I_{0}[\phi]
is the Newman–Penrose charge of
\phi
, defined as follows:

I_{0}[\phi]=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r_{*}})(r\phi)(\tau=0,r,\theta,\varphi)\,\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi.

The above limit is in fact well-defined for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and is moreover
conserved in
\tau
.

In the case
I_{0}[\phi]=0
; for example, when considering compactly supported initial data on
\tau=0
, Theorem
1.1
still holds with
\alpha=0
if we replace
f(\tau,r)
with
\partial_{\tau}f(\tau,r)
. In this case,
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]=4I_{0}^{(1)}[\phi]
, with
I^{(1)}_{0}[\phi]
the
time inverted Newman–Penrose charge
, which was first defined in
[
AAG18a
]
and is in fact equal to
I_{0}[\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi]
, where
\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi
is the
time integral of
\phi
, defined as follows:

\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=-\int^{\infty}_{\tau}\phi(s,r,\theta,\varphi)\,ds.

In both the
I_{0}[\phi]\neq 0
and
I_{0}[\phi]=0
cases, the mechanism developed in
[
AAG18a
,
AAG18b
]
for deriving late-time asymptotics relies on the conservation property of a Newman–Penrose charge at infinity, which is then propagated to the rest of the spacetime using appropriate decay estimates in time for
\phi
.

When considering projections of
\phi
onto all spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)
of degree
\ell
, denoted
\phi_{\ell}
, the Laplacian on the unit round sphere
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
, which appears in the wave operator
\square_{g}
, satisfies the following property:

\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi_{\ell}=-\ell(\ell+1)\phi_{\ell},

so we can interpret the term
r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi_{\ell}
appearing in
\square_{g_{M}}\phi_{\ell}
as a potential term
-V_{\alpha}\phi_{\ell}
, where
V_{\alpha}(r)=\alpha r^{-2}
with
\alpha=\ell(\ell+1)
, where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

In the setting of (
1.1
), the late-time asymptotics for
\phi_{\ell}
(and in fact for
\phi_{\geq\ell}
, the projections of
\phi
onto all spherical harmonics of degree
\geq\ell
), were obtained in
[
AAG21b
]
; see also earlier work on
\ell
-dependent decay estimates
[
DSS11
]
and on late-time asymptotics in compact regions in space
[
Hin22
]
.

These late-time asymptotics agree with Theorem
1.1
if we take
\alpha=\ell(\ell+1)
and we replace
w_{0}
with
P_{\ell}(r-M)
, the
\ell
-th Legendre polynomial. In this case,
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
will be proportional to
I_{\ell}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)
, with
I_{\ell}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)
the
\ell
-th Newman–Penrose charge, which is defined as a limit along
\tau=0
as
r\to\infty
of appropriate linear combinations of higher-order powers of derivatives
r^{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r_{*}})
acting on
r\phi
; see
[
GK22
]
for explicit expressions for
I_{\ell}[\phi]
. Moreover, for compactly supported data (
I_{\ell}[\phi]=0
),
f(\tau,r)
is replaced with
\partial_{\tau}f(\tau,r)
and
Q_{\alpha}[\phi]
is then proportional to
I_{\ell}[\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi]
.

When considering (
1.4
) with
\alpha=\ell(\ell+1)
, where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the conservation of
\ell
-th Newman–Penrose charges at infinity can therefore still be exploited to derive late-time asymptotics.

The numerology appearing in the decay rates of asymptotic inverse-polynomial tails in the setting of (
1.1
) (Price’s law) dates back to heuristic work in
[
Pri72
]
. The presence of inverse-polynomial late-time tails for (
1.1
) with sufficiently rapidly decaying data can be attributed to the non-vanishing of the mass
M
, which appears as a factor in the expressions for
I_{\ell}[\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi]
.
5
5

5

On a
3+1
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime background, which is locally isometric to Schwarzschild with
M=0
, the
strong Huygens principle
holds and there are no late-time tails in the case of compactly supported initial data; instead,
\phi
is compactly supported in
\tau
.

We note also the late-time asymptotics derived in
[
Keh21a
,
Keh21b
,
GK22
]
, where initial data are considered with various different
r
-asymptotics that arise from more physically motivated settings and are connected to the failure of peeling of curvature components for the Einstein equations. Such data require the use of
modified
Newman–Penrose charges to obtain late-time asymptotics.

In the setting of the present paper, where we consider (
1.4
) with
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we can no longer exploit conserved charges for
\phi
at infinity to obtain late-time asymptotics, so the methods involving Newman–Penrose charges do not apply, thus motivating the need for a new, more robust method. We refer to §
4.2
for an outline of this method.

When considering wave equations with non-trivial potentials, an important difference between potentials satisfying
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2-\delta}
, with
\delta>0
and asymptotically inverse-square potentials
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2}
, with
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, is that in the former case, the methods involving Newman–Penrose charges
can
still be applied as sketched above in the
V\equiv 0
setting to obtain late-time asymptotics with decay rates that are independent of
\alpha
. For example, in the former case we would expect
\phi|_{r=r_{0}}\sim\tau^{-\min\{3,2+\delta\}}
for compactly supported initial data. Recall that when
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2}
, we have instead:
\phi|_{r=r_{0}}\sim\tau^{-1-\sqrt{1+4\alpha}}
, so the leading-order coefficient
\alpha
appears in the decay rate, in contrast with the case where
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2-\delta}
.

Note moreover that in contrast with the
V\equiv 0
setting, both
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2}
with
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
,
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2-\delta}
with
\delta>0
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\mathbb{N}_{0}
, will lead to time integrals
\partial_{\tau}^{-1}\phi
that are
not
conformally smooth at null infinity, even if we start with smooth, compactly supported initial data. That is to say, time integrals will only have
finite regularity
with respect to the coordinate
x=\frac{1}{r}
at
x=0
(
r=\infty
). While this is perhaps not so surprising for
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2-\delta}=\alpha x^{2+\delta}
, which itself has only finite regularity at
x=0
, it is
a priori
perhaps not so obvious that there should also be a breakdown in conformal regularity when, for example,
V(r)=\alpha r^{-2}=\alpha x^{2}
, with
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
,
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, which
is
conformally smooth at
x=0
.

1.2
Inverse-square potentials as a toy model

The main motivation for considering geometric wave equations with inverse-square potentials in the present paper is to provide a simplified setting for tackling some of the difficulties that show up when studying the late-time behaviour and stability properties of solutions to the charged scalar field equation on black hole backgrounds and solutions to the (uncharged) wave equation on extremal Kerr spacetime backgrounds. The methods of the present paper can moreover be used to determine the late-time asymptotics of geometric wave equations corresponding to asymptotically flat spacetimes with even space dimensions. We briefly outline these different settings below.

Charged scalar fields

A natural generalization of the equation
\square_{g_{M}}\phi=0
to the setting of the Einstein–Maxwell equations is the equation:

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3}(g_{M,e}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}D_{\alpha}D_{\beta}\phi=

\displaystyle\>0,\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=

\displaystyle\>\nabla_{\mu}+iqA_{\mu},

\displaystyle dA=

\displaystyle\>F_{RN}=\frac{e}{r^{2}}dt\wedge dr,

where
e\in\mathbb{R}
is the electromagnetic charge appearing in the subextremal Reissner–Nordström black hole metric
g_{M,e}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
is the electromagnetic charge carried by the scalar field
\phi
and
F_{RN}
is a solution to the vacuum Maxwell-equations
d^{\star}F_{RN}=0
with respect to
g_{M,e}
.

The above equations show up in the linearization of the coupled system of Einstein–Maxwell-charged scalar field equations around the Reissner–Nordström solution, and they model the gravitational properties of eletromagnetic waves coupled to charged scalar field matter when keeping the Maxwell field and background spacetime metric fixed. After choosing an appropriate electromagnetic gauge
A_{\mu}
, the equation for
\phi
will resemble (
1.3
) with an inverse-square potential
V(r)\sim\alpha r^{-2}
where
\alpha<0
depends on the scalar field charge
q
, and hence, when determining the late-time asymptotics of
\phi
for the charged scalar field equation, we also encounter the difficulties faced in the setting of the wave equation on Schwarzschild with inverse-square potential. In particular, one cannot exploit conserved charges at null infinity to obtain late-time asymptotics.

In upcoming work
[
GVdM
]
, the methods developed in the present paper are applied to obtain the precise late-time asymptotics of charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström spacetimes under the assumption
q^{2}e^{2}<\frac{1}{4}
, which is analogous to the assumption
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
, and, in particular, the decay rates first suggested in
[
HP98
]
are validated for this range of charge parameters. In
[
GVdM22
]
, a different method is applied for deriving results on late-time asymptotics for charged scalar fields on Minkowski spacetime backgrounds that relies on the special conformal geometric properties of the Minkowski spacetime. We refer also to earlier work
[
VdM18
]
on decay estimates for the coupled Maxwell–charged scalar field system with small scalar field charge on a fixed Reissner–Nordström background.

Determining the late-time asymptotics of charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström is an important first step towards determining the late-time behaviour along the event horizon of dynamical black holes with charged matter, which is closely connected to the singularity properties in the corresponding black hole interiors and features some interesting new phenomena that do not feature in the uncharged setting; see for example
[
VdM19
,
KVdM21
]
.

Extremal black holes

The properties of solutions to the geometric wave equation on an extremal Kerr spacetime

\square_{g_{EK}}\phi=0

near the event horizon are closely related to the properties near null infinity, provided
\phi
is assumed to be axisymmetric. In particular, axisymmetric solutions also satisfy conservation laws along the event horizon of extremal Kerr black holes, which are directly related to the conservation laws for Newman–Penrose charges along future null infinity; see
[
CT84
,
LMRT13
,
BF13
]
. The presence of such conservation laws can not only be used to derive late-time asymptotics, see
[
AAG20
]
, but it is also the origin of the existence of axisymmetric asymptotic
instabilities
on extremal Kerr black holes
[
Are12
,
Are15
]
.

When considering
non
-axisymmetric solutions, however, the above relation between null infinity and the event horizon, and the corresponding conservation laws, break down. If we restrict to azimuthal modes, which are eigenfunctions of the axial vector field
\partial_{\varphi}
with eigenvalues
im
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is the azimuthal number, the wave equation near the event horizon instead resembles the equation for a charged scalar field with charge
q\sim m
near infinity. Therefore, the philosophy underlying the methods developed in the present paper, which relates (conformal) regularity of time integrals at
\tau=0
to decay rates in time, can also be applied to the setting of extremal Kerr. In upcoming work
[
Gaj
]
, we derive the late-time asymptotics for azumuthal modes on extremal Kerr and obtain as a corollary the existence of
azimuthal instabilities
, which are stronger than the axisymmetric instabilities mentioned above; see also
[
Gaj21
]
. We refer to
[
CGZ16
]
for earlier heuristic work on azimuthal instabilities and
[
TdC20
]
for a proof of mode stability on extremal Kerr.

Late-time tails of waves in even space dimensions

The wave equation on
n+1
-dimensional Schwarzschild spacetimes (or, the Schwarzschild–Tangherlini spacetimes
[
Tan63
]
) with
n\geq 3
takes the following form in standard Schwarzschild-type coordinates:

0=-D^{-1}\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+r^{-(n-1)}\partial_{r}(Dr^{n-1}\partial_{r}\phi)+r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\phi,

with
D=1-\frac{2M}{r^{n-2}}
and
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}
the Laplacian with respect to the unit round
n-1
-sphere metric; see for example
[
Sch13
,
Mos16
]
. Introducing the rescaled variable
\psi=r^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\phi
,
6
6

6

When evaluated at future null infinity,
\psi
corresponds to the
Friedlander radiation field
in
n
space dimensions.
the above equation is equivalent to:

0=-D^{-1}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi+\partial_{r}(D\partial_{r}\psi)-\frac{D}{4r^{2}}(n-1)(n-3)\psi-\frac{n-1}{2r}\frac{dD}{dr}\psi+r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\psi.

The term
-\frac{D}{4r^{2}}(n-1)(n-3)\psi
can be interpreted as a potential term:

-V_{\alpha}(r)\psi,

with
V_{\alpha}(r)=\alpha r^{-2}+O_{\infty}(r^{-n})
. When
n=2k+3
, with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have that
\alpha=k(k+1)
. Hence, one can obtain late-time asymptotics and sharp decay estimates for
\phi
by exploiting the existence of conserved charges at infinity that are analogous to those for fixed spherical harmonic projections
\phi_{\ell}
in the
n=3
case; see the discussion in §
1.1
.

When
n=2k+4
, with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have that
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and one can no longer make use of conservation laws at infinity. In the case of the
n+1
-dimensional wave equation on Minkowski (
M=0
), this means that the strong Huygens principle does not hold in even space dimensions
n
. The method for deriving late-time asymptotics introduced in the present paper, however, does remain applicable in this case and can be used to derive a higher-dimensional analogue of Price’s law.

Note that when considering the
n+1
-dimensional wave equation on Minkowski with
n=2
, and taking
\psi=r^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi
, we see an effective potential term with potential
V_{\alpha}(r)=\alpha r^{-2}
with
\alpha=-\frac{1}{4}
. As the methods in the present paper rely on the restriction
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
, they do not apply directly in this setting. It would therefore be an interesting problem to extend the methods to include the case
\alpha=-\frac{1}{4}
. See
[
DK16
]
for sharp decay estimates for wave equations with potentials on the real (half-)line corresponding to the choice
\alpha=-\frac{1}{4}
, which are derived by using Fourier transforms that are adapted to the potential.

We finally note that wave equations with inverse-square potentials also show up in the study of wave equations on manifolds with asymptotically conical ends; see
[
SSS10a
,
SSS10b
]
.

1.3
Outline of paper

Here, we give an outline of the structure of the remainder of the paper.

In §§
2
–
4
, we give precise formulations of the main results, after introducing the preliminary geometric objects and notations needed to understand the formulations. We moreover introduce the key ideas and the main steps of the proofs here. More precisely:

•

In §
2
we derive preliminary geometric properties of the Schwarzschild spacetime backgrounds that will play a role in the remainder of the paper. We moreover introduce the main notational conventions.

•

Then in §
3
, we introduce precisely the class of wave equations with potentials will be analyzed.

•

We give precise formulations of the main theorems of the paper in §
4
and already give a sketch of their proofs, emphasizing the key new ideas introduced in the present paper.

The full proofs of the results stated in §
4
are then carried out in §§
5
–
11
. These sections are structured as follows:

•

In §
5
, we derive energy boundedness estimates.

•

We derive integrated energy decay estimates in §
6
.

•

In §
7
, we derive (commuted)
r
-weighted energy estimates.

•

Then in §
8
, we combine the estimates in §§
5
–
7
to derive energy decay-in-time estimates for time derivatives.

•

We construct time integrals and develop an elliptic theory of time inversion in §
9
.

•

The energy decay-in-time estimates in §
8
are combined with the time-inversion theory in §
9
to derive the main result of the paper in §
10
: uniform, pointwise, decay-in-time estimates from which we can read off the precise late-time asymptotics.

•

We conclude in §
11
by showing how the results in §
10
also give late-time asymptotics for solutions supported on spherical harmonics with higher angular momenta
\ell
.

1.4
Acknowledgements

The author thanks Leonhard Kehrberger for comments on an earlier version of the manuscript.

2
Preliminaries: geometry and foliations

In this section, we introduce the main geometric concepts and notation that will be used throughout the paper.

2.1
Spacetime metric and foliations

Let
M>0
and consider the manifold-with-boundary
(\mathcal{M}_{M},g_{M})
with:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{M}=

\displaystyle\>\mathbb{R}_{v}\times[2M,\infty)_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\theta,\varphi},

equipped with a coordinate chart
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
and the metric:

\displaystyle g_{M}=

\displaystyle\>-Ddv^{2}+2dvdr+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}),

\displaystyle D(r)=

\displaystyle\>1-\frac{2M}{r}.

The coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
are standard spherical coordinates on the 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
that degenerate at a line connecting the two poles. The spacetimes
(\mathcal{M}_{M},g_{M})
with
M>0
are called
Schwarzschild black hole exteriors
.

We define the functions

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\>v-r_{*},

\displaystyle u=

\displaystyle\>v-2r_{*},\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle r_{*}=

\displaystyle\>\int_{3M}^{r}D^{-1}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}.

Note that the level sets of the coordinate function
v
define ingoing null hypersurfaces, whereas the level sets of
u
define outgoing null hypersurfaces. Furthermore, by considering coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates we recover the standard form of the Schwarzschild metric in the subset
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(2M,\infty)_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\theta,\varphi}
, see (
1.2
).

Let
\tilde{h}:[2M,\infty)\to(0,2]
be a smooth function such that

2-\tilde{h}D>0.

Define moreover
h:[2M,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
as follows:

h(r):=2-\tilde{h}(r)D(r).

Consider the time function
\tau
:

\tau=v-v_{0}-\int_{2M}^{r}\tilde{h}\,dr^{\prime},

where
v_{0}>0
.

We then have that

(g_{M})^{-1}(d\tau,d\tau)=-\tilde{h}h<0,

so the level sets of
\tau
,

\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}=\{\tau=\tau^{\prime}\},

are spacelike hypersurfaces.

We further restrict to an
asymptotically hyperboloidal
foliation
\Sigma_{\tau}
by imposing

\displaystyle h(r)=

\displaystyle\>h_{0}r^{-2}+O_{\infty}(r^{-3}),

with
h_{0}
a constant, which is necessarily positive, by the previous assumption on
\tilde{h}
.

Here we applied the following variation of “big-O notation”: we write
f(r)=O_{k}(r^{-q})
for a suitably regular function
f:[2M,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|\partial_{r}^{l}f|\leq Cr^{-q-l}
for all
0\leq l\leq k
. If
f=O_{k}(r^{-q})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we write
f=O_{\infty}(r^{-q})
and we write
O(r^{-q})=~O_{0}(r^{-q})
.

We denote with

\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\tau\in[0,\infty)}\Sigma_{\tau}

the spacetime subset of
\mathcal{M}_{M}
that will be relevant for the analysis in this paper.

Note that
\Sigma_{\tau}=F_{\tau}(\Sigma_{0})
, where
F_{\tau}
denotes the flow along the vector field
T=\partial_{v}
, which is the standard timelike Killing vector field in Schwarzschild. From the Killing property of
T
, it follows that the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
are all isometric. We will moreover denote
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{0}
.

The null coordinate
u
takes on the following values along
\Sigma_{\tau}
,

u|_{\Sigma_{0}}(r)=v|_{\Sigma_{0}}(r)-2r_{*}=\tau+\left[v_{0}+\int_{2M}^{3M}\tilde{h}\,dr^{\prime}\right]-\int_{3M}^{r}h\,D^{-1}dr^{\prime},

so

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}u|_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\tau+u_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle u_{0}=

\displaystyle\>\left[v_{0}+\int_{2M}^{3M}\tilde{h}\,dr^{\prime}\right]-\int_{3M}^{\infty}h\,D^{-1}dr^{\prime},

where the above expression for
u_{0}
is well-defined because
h
is integrable in
r
, by the assumptions on the
r
-decay of
h
(asymptotical hyperbolicity).

The triple
(r,\theta,\varphi)
defines a coordinate chart on the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
, which can be complemented with the time function
\tau
to obtain the coordinate chart
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
on
\mathcal{R}
. We can express
g_{M}
as follows in these coordinates:

g_{M}=-Dd\tau^{2}-2(1-h)d\tau dr+h\tilde{h}dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}).

We consider now the rescaled metric
\widehat{g}_{M}=r^{-2}g_{M}
and employ the coordinate
x=\frac{1}{r}
to express:

\widehat{g}_{M}=-x^{2}d\tau^{2}+2(1-h)d\tau dx+r^{2}h\tilde{h}dx^{2}+d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}.

By assumption,
r^{2}h:[0,(2M)^{-1})_{x}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defines a smooth function of
x
, so we can smoothly
7
7

7

The smoothness of the extension is actually not necessary to be able to apply the methods in this paper, since we only make use of
finite
regularity properties.
extend the Lorentzian manifold-with-boundary
([0,\infty)_{\tau}\times(0,\frac{1}{2M}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\widehat{g}_{M})
to
([0,\infty)_{\tau}\times[0,\frac{1}{2M}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\widehat{g}_{M})
, where we denote:

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}=\left[0,\frac{1}{2M}\right]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}.

We similarly denote with
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
the union of
\Sigma_{\tau}
with the appropriate level set of
\tau
in the extended manifold-with-boundary
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
.

We denote

\mathcal{I}^{+}=\{x=0\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{R}}

and refer to this hypersurface as
future null infinity
.

Note that we can express
T=\partial_{\tau}
. It will be convenient to denote moreover

X=\partial_{r}

in
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates. In
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, on the other hand, we can express:
T=\partial_{v}
,
X=\partial_{r}+\tilde{h}\partial_{v}
.

Note that

\displaystyle\underline{L}=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{D}{2}X+\frac{D}{2}\tilde{h}T\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}

(2.1)

\displaystyle L=

\displaystyle\>T-\underline{L}=\frac{D}{2}X+\frac{1}{2}hT

(2.2)

define ingoing and outgoing null vector fields, respectively.
8
8

8

In
(u,v,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, we can express
\underline{L}=\partial_{u}
and
L=\partial_{v}
.

We also denote

Z=L-\underline{L}=DX+(h-1)T.

(2.3)

Finally, we let
\mathbf{N}
be a smooth vector field, such that
\mathbf{N}=T
for
r\geq 4M
and
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{n}_{\tau}
for
r\leq 3M
, where
\mathbf{n}_{\tau}
denotes the normal vector field to
\Sigma_{\tau}

Figure 1
:
A 2-dimensional representation of the spacetime
\mathcal{M}_{M}
, with the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
and the shaded region depicting
\mathcal{R}
. Each point in the picture represents a round 2-sphere of radius
r
and the hypersurface
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, which represents the points
(\tau,\infty,\theta,\varphi)
, is depicted at a finite distance.

2.2
Properties of functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}

Let
Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)
denote spherical harmonics on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, labelled by an angular momentum
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and an azimuthal number
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|m|\leq\ell
. We can define projection operators
\pi_{\ell}:C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})\to C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})
as follows:

(\pi_{\ell}f)(r,\theta,\varphi):=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\left[\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f(r,\theta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})Y_{\ell m}(\theta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\,\sin\theta^{\prime}d\theta^{\prime}d\varphi^{\prime}\right]Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)

and extend
\pi_{\ell}
as a bounded linear operator to
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})
(with respect to the standard volume form on
\Sigma_{\tau}
given the induced metric on
\Sigma_{\tau}
).

We denote the
f_{\ell}=\pi_{\ell}f
and refer to
f_{\ell}
as a spherical harmonic mode. Since the functions
Y_{\ell m}
make up an orthonormal basis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, we can express:

f=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}f_{\ell}.

We moreover denote for
\ell_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f_{\geq\ell_{0}}=\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}f_{\ell}
.

Define the angular momentum operators
\Omega_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
as follows:

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}=

\displaystyle-\sin\varphi\partial_{\theta}-\cot\theta\cos\varphi\partial_{\varphi},

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}=

\displaystyle\>\cos\varphi\partial_{\theta}-\cot\theta\sin\varphi\partial_{\varphi},

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\varphi}.

It is straightforward to show that
[\Omega_{i},\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}]=0
, where
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}(\cdot))+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}(\cdot)
denotes the Laplacian on the unit round sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

We denote with
H^{k}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
the
k
-th order
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with respect to the volume form
d\sigma:=\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f\in H^{k}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Then:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f|^{2}\,d\sigma=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{|I|=1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{I}f)^{2}\,d\sigma,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{I}f)^{2}\,d\sigma\leq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}f|^{2}\,d\sigma\leq

\displaystyle\>\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{I}f)^{2}\,d\sigma,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}f_{\geq\ell}|^{2}\,d\sigma\geq

\displaystyle\>(\ell(\ell+1))^{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{\geq\ell}^{2}\,d\sigma,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}f_{\ell}|^{2}\,d\sigma=

\displaystyle\>(\ell(\ell+1))^{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{\ell}^{2}\,d\sigma,

(2.7)

where we used multi-index notation
\Omega^{I}=\Omega^{I_{1}}\ldots\Omega^{I_{n}}
for
I=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
.

Furthermore, if
f\in H^{2k}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, then

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}f)^{2}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{2k}f|^{2}\,d\sigma.

(2.8)

and if
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, then there exist a numerical constant
C>0
, such that

||f||_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\leq C||f||_{H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}.

(2.9)

Proof.

The estimates (
2.6
)–(
2.8
) follow simply by using that
f=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}f_{\ell}
, restricting to
f_{\ell}
, making use of the identity
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{\ell}=-\ell(\ell+1)f_{\ell}
and integrating by parts.

We obtain (
2.4
) by applying the expressions for
\Omega_{i}
above. For (
2.5
), it is convenient to restrict to
f_{\ell}
. Using (
2.4
), we obtain:

\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{I}f_{\ell})^{2}\,d\sigma\leq\sum_{|J_{k}|=1}\ldots\sum_{|J_{1}|=1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{J_{k}}\ldots\Omega^{J_{1}}f_{\ell})^{2}\,d\sigma=(\ell(\ell+1))^{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{\ell}^{2}\,d\sigma.

Similarly,

(\ell(\ell+1))^{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{\ell}^{2}\,d\sigma=\sum_{|\beta_{k}|=1}\ldots\sum_{|J_{1}|=k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{J_{k}}\Omega^{J_{1}}f_{\ell})^{2}\,d\sigma\leq k!\sum_{|I|=k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\Omega^{I}f_{\ell})^{2}\,d\sigma.

We then apply (
2.7
) and sum over
\ell
to obtain (
2.5
). The inequality (
2.9
) is a standard Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, so the proof will be omitted.
∎

2.3
A Hardy inequality

Throughout the paper, we will frequently make use of the following Hardy inequality:

Lemma 2.2

(Hardy inequality)
.

Let
f\in C^{1}([a,b])
with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
a<b
. Then for
q\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{-1\}
:

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}x^{q}|f|^{2}(x)\,dx\leq\frac{2}{q+1}\left[b^{q+1}|f|^{2}(b)-a^{q+1}|f|^{2}(a)\right]+\frac{4}{(q+1)^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}x^{q+2}\left|\frac{df}{dx}\right|^{2}(x)\,dx.

(2.10)

Proof.

Integrate
\frac{d}{dx}(x^{q+1}|f|^{2})
, apply the fundamental theorem of calculus and apply a (weighted) Young’s inequality to estimate:

\left|2x^{q+1}f\frac{df}{dx}\right|\leq\frac{|q+1|}{2}x^{q}|f|^{2}+\frac{2}{|q+1|}x^{q+2}\left|\frac{df}{dx}\right|^{2}.

∎

2.4
Additional notation

We denote with
d\mu_{\tau}
the standard volume form corresponding to the induced metric
i^{*}_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g_{M}
along
\Sigma_{\tau}
, with
i_{\Sigma_{\tau}}:\Sigma_{\tau}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{R}
the inclusion map.

Throughout the paper we will frequently use
C
or
c
to denote positive constants depending only on
M,h
and
V_{\alpha}
. In order to make the notation more compact, we will not keep precise track of constants and we will instead make use of the following “algebra of constants” when constants
c
or
C
appear in an equality:

C+C=C\cdot C=C,c+c=c\cdot c=c.

We will moreover use the notations
f\lesssim g
and
f\sim g
, with
f,g
non-negative functions, to mean respectively:

f\leq Cg,\hskip 10.00002ptcg\leq f\leq Cg.

3
Preliminaries: wave equations

In this section, we introduce the wave equations that are studied in the present paper, and we derive their global existence, uniqueness and regularity properties.

3.1
Class of wave equations with potentials

Let
V_{\alpha}:[2M,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function, such that:

\displaystyle r^{2}V_{\alpha}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\alpha+O_{\infty}(r^{-1}),

with
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
.

We additionally make the following assumption:

A)

V_{\alpha}
satisfies the condition:

V_{\alpha}(r)>-\frac{1}{4r^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for all $r\geq 2M$}.

In fact, it is possible to consider the alternative assumption:

A’)

The operator

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}=\frac{d}{dr}\left(Dr^{2}\frac{d}{dr}(\cdot)\right)-[r^{2}V_{\alpha}+\ell(\ell+1)](\cdot)

has no
no zero-energy resonances
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
: there do not exist smooth solutions
w_{\ell}
to the ODE
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}w_{\ell}=0
that satisfy outgoing boundary conditions, i.e. that behave asymptotically like the most rapidly decaying branch of ODE solutions as
r\to\infty
.

Assumption
A)
ensures coercivity of the energy corresponding to the vector field
T
; see Proposition
5.1
. In Lemma
3.3
below, it is shown that assumption
A)
implies assumption
A’)
. For the sake of convenience, we will assume the stronger assumption
A)
throughout the rest of the paper, as it allows us to employ more straightforward
T
-energy estimates.

In this article, we will focus on the equations:

\square_{g_{M}}\phi=V_{\alpha}\phi

(3.1)

in
\mathcal{R}
, with
\square_{g_{M}}
denoting the Laplace–Beltrami operator with respect to
g_{M}
.

We introduce the following rescaled function that will play an important role in the remainder of the article:

\psi:=r\phi.

The limit
\psi|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
is known as the
Friedlander radiation field
.

In the lemma below we denote various different forms of the wave equation with potential (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\phi
be a solution to (
3.1
). Then:

0=X(Dr^{2}X\phi)-2r^{2}(1-h)XT\phi-r^{2}h\tilde{h}T^{2}\phi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi-r^{2}V_{\alpha}\phi+\left(\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}h\right)-2r\right)T\phi.

(3.2)

Furthermore, (
3.1
) is equivalent to the following equations for
\psi
:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\>X(DX\psi)-2(1-h)XT\psi-h\tilde{h}T^{2}\psi+r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi+\frac{dh}{dr}T\psi-V_{\alpha}\psi-D^{\prime}r^{-1}\psi,

(3.3)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\>-4\underline{L}L\psi+Dr^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-D(V_{\alpha}+D^{\prime}r^{-1})\psi,

(3.4)

and we can express in
(\tau,x,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates:

0=\partial_{x}(x^{2}\partial_{x}\psi)+2(1-h)\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi-r^{2}h(2-h)\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi+\frac{dh}{dr}\partial_{\tau}\psi-r^{2}V_{\alpha}\psi-D^{\prime}r\psi.

(3.5)

Proof.

The equations are straightforward computations, which follow from writing:

\square_{g_{M}}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\det g_{M}}}\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3}\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-\det g_{M}}(g^{-1}_{M})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}(\cdot)\right)

and making use of the relations between the different vector fields in (
2.1
)–(
2.3
).
∎

3.2
Global existence, uniqueness and regularity at null infinity

We establish below global existence and uniqueness for the initial value problem for (
3.1
) with initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
. We moreover show that regularity of the initial data with respect to the conformal manifold
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
is propagated.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(i)

Consider the initial data functions
(\phi_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{i})\in H^{k+1}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0})\times H^{k}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0})
. There exists a unique solution solution
\phi
to (
3.1
) in
\mathcal{R}
(in a weak sense), such that for all
T_{*}\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\phi\in

\displaystyle\>C^{0}([0,T_{*}],H^{k+1}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0}))\cap C^{1}([0,T_{*}],H^{k}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0})),

\displaystyle\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\phi_{i},

\displaystyle T\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\phi^{\prime}_{i}.

Here the Sobolev spaces are defined with respect to the standard volume form on
\Sigma_{0}
.

(ii)

If we moreover assume that
(r\phi_{i},r\phi^{\prime}_{i})\in H^{k+1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{0})\times H^{k}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{0})
. Then, for all
\tau\geq 0
,

(\psi|_{\Sigma_{\tau}},T\psi|_{\Sigma_{\tau}})\in H^{k+1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau})\times H^{k}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau}).

(3.6)

Here the Sobolev spaces are defined with respect to the standard volume form on
\widehat{\Sigma}_{0}
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
follows from standard global existence and uniqueness arguments for linear wave equations, so we will only sketch the proof:

1.

We establish first an energy estimate in
\bigcup_{\tau\in[0,T_{*}]}\Sigma_{\tau}
, with constants depending on
T_{*}
, by integrating by parts
\mathbf{N}\phi\square_{g}\phi
and then repeating the argument with higher-order energies, by integrating by parts
(-1)^{k}\mathbf{N}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}X^{j}T^{l}\phi\square_{g}X^{j}T^{l}\phi
.

2.

We apply a functional analytic argument, using the above energy estimates to establish the existence of weak solutions; see for example §I.3 of
[
Sog08
]
,

3.

We improve the regularity of the solutions; see for example §I.3 of
[
Sog08
]
.

In order to obtain part
(ii)
, we instead work with the equation (
3.5
) and apply the arguments for global existence and uniqueness as above, using that the coefficients in (
3.5
) are sufficiently regular with respect to the differentiable structure on the manifold-with-boundary
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
.
∎

3.3
Definition of stationary weight functions

The following number will play a fundamental role in the decay rates and late-time asymptotics of
\phi
:

\beta_{\ell}=\sqrt{1+4\alpha+4\ell(\ell+1)}.

The number
\beta_{\ell}
arises from the asymptotic behaviour of stationary solutions to (
3.1
), as demonstrated in Lemma
3.3
below, where we derive the existence, uniqueneness and
r\to\infty
asymptotics of stationary solutions.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
V_{\alpha}
satisfy assumption
A)
. There exists a unique smooth solution
w_{\ell}:[2M,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
to

(Dr^{2}w_{\ell}^{\prime})^{\prime}-[r^{2}V_{\alpha}+\ell(\ell+1)]w_{\ell}=0,

(3.7)

such that

w_{\ell}(r)=r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}+O_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}})+O_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}).

(3.8)

The functions
w_{\ell}\cdot Y_{\ell m}
with
|m|\leq\ell
are stationary solutions to (
3.1
) (i.e. solutions with vanishing
T
-derivative).

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

When
V_{\alpha}\equiv 0
, we have that
w_{\ell}(r)=P_{\ell}(r-M)
, with
P_{\ell}(y)
the
\ell
-th Legendre polynomial in
y
. Furthermore, in this case
w_{\ell}(r)\sim r^{\ell}
as
r\to\infty
.

We introduce the following
renormalization
of the spherical harmonic modes
\phi_{\ell}
:

\check{\phi}_{\ell}=w_{\ell}^{-1}\phi_{\ell},

In the lemma below, we obtain equations for the renormalized functions
\check{\phi}_{\ell}

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\phi
be a solution to (
3.1
). Then
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\>Z^{2}\check{\phi}_{\ell}+2(r^{-1}+w_{\ell}^{\prime}w_{\ell}^{-1})Z\check{\phi}_{\ell}-T^{2}\check{\phi}_{\ell},

(3.9)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\>X(Dr^{2}w_{\ell}^{2}X\check{\phi}_{\ell})-2r^{2}w_{\ell}^{2}(1-h)XT\check{\phi}_{\ell}-h\tilde{h}r^{2}w_{\ell}^{2}T^{2}\check{\phi}_{\ell}-(w_{\ell}^{2}r^{2}(1-h))^{\prime}T\check{\phi}_{\ell}.

(3.10)

Proof.

Apply (
3.2
) in combination with (
3.7
) to obtain (
3.10
). Then (
3.9
) follows by using (
2.3
). ∎

Remark 3.2
.

The choice of renormalization
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
is made so that the equations satisfied by
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
contain only
T
- and
X
-derivatives of
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
, i.e. they do not feature zeroth-order terms in
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
.

4
The main results and ideas

In this section, we give a precise formulation of the main results, and we outline the key new ideas that play a role in the corresponding proofs.

4.1
Precise statements of the main results

Relevant initial data quantities

In order to give precise statements of the main results, we introduce the following initial data quantities: recall that

\beta_{\ell}=\sqrt{1+4\alpha+4\ell(\ell+1)}.

Then the assumption that
\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}
and
\alpha\neq l(l+1)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, implies that
\beta_{\ell}\neq 2l+1
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We moreover introduce:

n_{\beta_{\ell}}=\left\lfloor\frac{\beta_{\ell}+1}{2}\right\rfloor,

and denote for
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\delta>0
:
9
9

9

Here,
d\tau^{\sharp}
is the vector field dual to the 1-form
d\tau
, with respect to the metric
g_{M}
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}[\phi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\beta_{0}}{2\beta_{0}}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\left[r(d\tau)^{\sharp}(r\phi)+\frac{dh}{dr}r^{2}\phi-r(1-h)X(r\phi)\right]w_{0}d\sigma dr,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\beta_{\ell}}{2\beta_{\ell}}\sum_{|m|\leq\ell}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\left[r(d\tau)^{\sharp}(r\phi)+\frac{dh}{dr}r^{2}\phi-r(1-h)X(r\phi)\right]\cdot Y_{\ell m}w_{\ell}d\sigma dr\cdot Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell}[\phi]=

\displaystyle\>\frac{(-1)^{n_{\beta_{\ell}}}n_{\beta_{\ell}}!}{2^{n_{\beta_{\ell}}-\beta_{\ell}}\pi}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\beta_{\ell}+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\beta_{\ell}-1}{2}-n_{\beta_{\ell}}\right)}{\Gamma(\beta_{\ell}-n_{\beta_{\ell}})}\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi],

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{N}}[\phi]=

\displaystyle\>\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{2}|X\phi|^{2}+h\tilde{h}r^{2}|T\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi|^{2}+|\phi|^{2}\,d\sigma dr,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{0},N,\delta}[\phi]=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{n\leq N+2}\left(||((r+1)^{2}(rX)^{n}X(r\phi_{0})||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}+||(rX)^{n}\phi_{0}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}+||r(rX)^{n}T\phi_{0}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}\leq N+2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{3-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}X(rX)^{n_{2}}\widehat{\psi}_{\geq 1}|^{2}+r^{-1-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}(rX)^{n_{2}}T\widehat{\psi}_{\geq 1}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}\leq 2N+3\\
n_{1}+n_{2}\leq N+2\end{subarray}}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{\min\{3\beta_{0},1\}-\delta}|L(rL)^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}\widehat{\psi}_{0}|^{2}+r^{1-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}L(rL)^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}-1}{\psi}_{\geq 1}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 3N+5\\
n_{1}\leq N+2\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{N}}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}T^{n_{2}}\widehat{\phi}]+\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}^{2}[\phi]\hskip 20.00003pt(\beta_{0}<1),

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{\ell},N,\delta}[\phi_{\geq\ell}]=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{n\leq N+3n_{\beta_{\ell}}+2}\Biggl(||(r+1)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}}(rX)^{n}X(r\phi_{\ell})||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}

\displaystyle+||r^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}}(rX)^{n}\phi_{\ell}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}+||r^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}}(rX)^{n}T\phi_{\ell}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}\Biggr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}\leq N+2n_{\beta}+2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{2+\min\{2n_{\beta_{\ell}}+1,\beta_{\ell+1}\}-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}X(rX)^{n_{2}}\widehat{\psi}_{\geq\ell+1}|^{2}

\displaystyle+r^{-2+\min\{2n_{\beta_{\ell}}+1,\beta_{\ell+1}\}-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}X(rX)^{n_{2}}T\widehat{\psi}_{\geq\ell+1}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}\leq 2N+3n_{\beta_{\ell}}+5\\
n_{1}+n_{2}\leq N+2n_{\beta_{\ell}}+2\end{subarray}}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}L(rL)^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}\widehat{\psi}_{\ell}|^{2}

\displaystyle+r^{\min\{\beta_{\ell+1}-2n_{\beta_{\ell}},1\}-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}L(rL)^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}{\psi}_{\geq\ell+1}|^{2}+r^{2+\min\{\beta_{\ell+1}-2n_{\beta_{\ell}},0\}-\delta}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}L(rL)^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}{\psi}_{\geq\ell+1}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 3N+5n_{\beta_{\ell}}+5\\
n_{1}\leq N+2n_{\beta_{\ell}}+2\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{N}}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}T^{n_{2}}\widehat{\phi}_{\geq\ell}]+\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}^{2}[\phi_{\geq\ell}]\hskip 20.00003pt(\beta_{0}>1\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{or}\hskip 10.00002pt\ell\geq 1)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where},

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}_{\geq\ell}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\>\phi_{\geq\ell}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)-\Phi^{(\beta_{\ell})}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)v\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\beta_{\ell})}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\>\Phi_{\ell}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)w_{\ell}(r)(\tau+2r)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}}(\tau+M)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}}.

Main theorem

We can now give precise formulations of the main results.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\delta>0
be arbitrarily small,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and let
\phi
be a solution to (
3.1
) with initial data satisfying
\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2}\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{0},N,\delta}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\phi]<~\infty
.

(i)

(Leading-order late-time asymptotics)
There exists a constant
C=C(M,h,V_{\alpha},N,\delta)>0
such that:

\displaystyle||T^{N}\phi-\Phi_{0}[\phi]w_{0}(r)T^{N}f||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\>Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}}f(\tau,r)(\tau+M)^{-\frac{1}{2}\min\{2\beta_{0},1-\beta_{0}+n_{\beta_{0}},\beta_{1}-\beta_{0}\}-N+\delta}\sqrt{\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2}\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{0},N,\delta}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\phi]},

\displaystyle f(\tau,r)=(\tau+2r)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}}(\tau+M)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}}.

(4.2)

(ii)

(Refined late-time asymptotics for higher spherical harmonics)
Let
\phi_{\geq\ell}
be a solution to (
3.1
) supported on spherical harmonics of degree
\geq\ell
, with initial data satisfying
\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2}\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{\ell},N,\delta}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\phi_{\geq\ell}]<\infty
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(M,h,V_{\alpha},N,\delta,\ell)>0
such that:

\displaystyle||T^{N}\phi_{\geq\ell}-\Phi_{\ell}[\phi_{\geq\ell}]w_{\ell}(r)T^{N}f_{\ell}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\>Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}f_{\ell}(\tau,r)(\tau+M)^{-\min\{n_{\beta_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{\ell}-1),\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{\ell+1}-\beta_{\ell})\}-N+\delta}\sqrt{\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2}\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{\ell},N,\delta}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\phi_{\geq\ell}]},

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(\tau,r)=(\tau+2r)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(\tau+M)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}.

(4.4)

(iii)

(Genericity of late-time tails)
Let
(\phi_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{i})=(\phi,T\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}}
denote initial data for
\phi
on
\Sigma_{0}
and consider:

\mathcal{D}=\left\{((\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell},(\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell}^{\prime})\>\textnormal{measurable}\,|\,\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2}\mathbf{D}_{\beta_{\ell},0,\delta}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\phi_{\geq\ell}]<\infty\right\}.

Then the subset

\mathcal{G}=\left\{((\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell},(\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}\,|\,\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi]\neq 0\right\}

is open and dense with respect to the weighted
L^{1}
-type norm:

||((\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell},(\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell}^{\prime})||_{\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\left[r|X(r(\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell})|+r^{-1}|(\phi_{i})_{\geq\ell}|+|(\phi^{\prime}_{i})_{\geq\ell}|\right]w_{\ell}\,d\sigma dr

and the complement
\mathcal{G}^{c}
has codimension
2\ell+1
. The above statements holds also if we replace
\mathcal{D}
above with the smaller data set
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})
.

The estimates (
4.1
) and (
4.3
) therefore give the precise leading-order late-time behaviour of
\phi_{\geq\ell}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, proving existence of inverse-polynomial late-time tails, if the initial data lie in
\mathcal{G}
, which is generic in the above sense.

(iv)

(Data of time integrals)
For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the
(n_{\beta_{\ell}}+1)
-th order time integrals

\begin{split}T^{-1-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\phi_{\geq\ell}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=&(-1)^{1+n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{\tau_{n_{\beta_{\ell}}}}^{\infty}\phi_{\geq\ell}(s,r,\theta,\varphi)\,dsd\tau_{n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\ldots d\tau_{1}\\
=&\>-\frac{1}{n_{\beta_{\ell}}!}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}(\tau-s)^{n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\phi(s,r,\theta,\varphi)\,ds.\end{split}

are well-defined and satisfy:

\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\phi_{\ell})(0,r,\theta,\varphi)=\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi](\theta,\varphi).

Part
(i)
of Theorem
4.1
is proved as part of Proposition
10.10
and part
(ii)
is proved as part of Proposition
11.1
. We conclude part
(iii)
by using that
\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi_{\ell m}]
with
|m|\leq\ell
, defined via integrals on
\Sigma_{0}
, are well-defined as linearly independent bounded linear functionals on the initial data space
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the norm
||(\cdot,\cdot)||_{\mathcal{D}}
. Their vanishing immediately implies that
\mathcal{G}^{c}
has codimension
2\ell+1
and is closed. Density follows easily by letting
(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}^{c}
and taking a sequence of smooth compactly supported initial data
(\phi_{i}^{(k)},{\phi^{\prime}}_{i}^{(k)})
such that
\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi^{(k)}_{\geq\ell}]\neq 0
and
||((\phi_{i}^{(k)})_{\geq\ell},({\phi^{\prime}}_{i}^{(k)})_{\geq\ell})||_{\mathcal{D}}\to 0
, and then considering
(\phi_{i}+\phi_{i}^{(k)},\phi_{i}+{\phi^{\prime}}_{i}^{(k)})
with
(\phi_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{i})\in\mathcal{G}^{c}
. Part
(iv)
follows from a combination of Proposition
9.11
and Corollary
11.2
.

Remark 4.1
.

Although the proof of Theorem
4.1
does not make use of conserved charges along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, one can
a posteriori
identify the following conserved quantities in
\tau
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
at the level of (multiple) time integrals:

\displaystyle r^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\phi_{\ell})|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}(\tau,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{if $\beta_{\ell}<3$},

\displaystyle r^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}X((r^{2}X)^{n_{\beta_{\ell}}}rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{\ell}}}\phi_{\ell})|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}(\tau,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\>2^{-n_{\beta_{\ell}}+1}(3-\beta_{\ell})\cdot\ldots\cdot(2n_{\beta_{\ell}}-1-\beta_{\ell})\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{\ell}}[\phi](\theta,\varphi)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{if $\beta_{\ell}>3$}.

The proof of the above conservation property is omitted in the present paper, but it follows in a straightforward manner by taking the
T
-derivative of the expressions on the left-hand side above and applying (
3.1
) together with the decay-in-time estimates of Theorem
4.1
.

Remark 4.2
.

The methods leading to a proof of Theorem
4.1
also apply to the setting of initial data with slower decay towards future null infinity, which can lower the conformal regularity of time integrals and lead to late-time tails with
slower
decay. For examples illustrating this fact in the setting of (
1.1
), see
[
Keh21a
,
Keh21b
,
GK22
]
.

Conversely, for initial data in
\mathcal{G}^{c}
, the time integrals have higher conformal regularity at infinity and the methods will still apply, provided we consider additional time integrals, which translate into inverse polynomial late-time tails with
faster
decay. It is therefore expected that the subset of initial data which does not lead to
any
inverse polynomial late-time tail in the late-time asymptotics is in fact of infinite codimension.

4.2
Overview of proof and summary of new ideas

In this section, we list the main steps of the proof of Theorem
4.1
and we give a sketch of the relevant new ideas and tools that are developed in the present paper.

Step 0:
Energy boundedness

Given vector fields
V,W
, we denote with

\mathbb{T}[\phi](V,W)=V(\phi)W(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}g_{M}(V,W)\left[(g^{-1}_{M})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+V_{\alpha}\phi^{2}\right]

the energy-momentum tensor corresponding to the equation (
3.1
).

It follows from integrating
T\phi(\square_{g}-V_{\alpha})\phi
by parts in a suitable spacetime slab and using assumption
A)
on the potential that the following energy quantity that degenerates at the event horizon
r=2M
is coercive and uniformly bounded in time:

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(T,\mathbf{n}_{\tau})[\phi]\,d\sigma dr\sim\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)(X(r\phi))^{2}+(T\phi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi|^{2}+\phi^{2}\,d\sigma dr

To remove the degenerate factor
1-\frac{2M}{r}
on the right-hand side above, we apply the red-shift estimates introduced in
[
DR09
]
.
Step 0
is carried out in detail in §
5
.

Step 1:
Integrated energy decay estimates

The first significant step towards passing from energy boundedness to energy decay estimates in
\tau
is to establish appropriate control of the integral in time of appropriate energy quantities, i.e. we wish to control

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})[\phi]+\phi^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\sim\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(X(r\phi))^{2}+(T\phi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi|^{2}+\phi^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau.

•

Differences with
\square_{g_{M}}\phi=0

When taking
V_{\alpha}\equiv 0
in (
3.1
), it is possible to control the above quantity locally as follows for any
R>2M
:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})[\phi]+\phi^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{0}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[\phi]+r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[T\phi]\,d\sigma dr,

(4.5)

where the extra
T
-derivative on the right-hand side is necessary due to the presence of trapped null geodesics at the photon sphere at
r=3M
(see for example
[
Sbi15
]
). While it is possible to prove (
4.5
) for
\alpha\geq 0
, the
\alpha<0
case can behave significantly differently. In fact, it follows from Theorem
4.1
that a uniform estimate of the form (
4.5
) cannot possibly hold for
-\frac{1}{4}<\alpha\leq-\frac{3}{16}
, or equivalently,
0<\beta_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

To see this, we consider for the sake of simplicity spherically symmetric, smooth and compactly supported data on
\Sigma_{0}
and take
-\frac{1}{4}<\alpha\leq-\frac{3}{16}
. Then, by
(iv)
of Theorem
4.1
,
r^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}}X(rT^{-1}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}}
attains a finite, non-zero limit at infinity which is equal to
\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}[\phi]
. In particular,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\leq R\}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[T^{-1}\phi]+r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[T(T^{-1}\phi)]\,d\sigma dr<

\displaystyle\>\infty.

(4.6)

Suppose now that (
4.5
) is true for
\phi
arising from initial data in the closure of smooth compactly supported functions under the energy norm on the right-hand side of (
4.5
). Then, by a density argument, using (
4.6
), the estimate (
4.5
) would also hold with
\phi
replaced by
T^{-1}\phi
, so we would in particular have that

\int_{0}^{\infty}(T^{-1}\phi)^{2}|_{r=r_{0}}\,d\tau<\infty

for any
2M\leq r_{0}<\infty
.
10
10

10

We control the integral of
\phi^{2}
along
r=r_{0}
by integrating
\partial_{r}(\chi\phi^{2})
from
r=r_{0}
to
r=r_{0}+M
, with
\chi
a smooth cut-off function which vanishes for
r\geq r_{0}+M
such that
\chi(r_{0})=1
, and applying (
4.5
).
However, integrating the late-time asymptotics in
(i)
of Theorem
4.1
in time to obtain late-time asymptotics for
T^{-1}\phi
, we also have that

T^{-1}\phi|_{r=r_{0}}=-\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}\Phi_{0}[\phi]w_{0}(r_{0})(\tau+M)^{-\beta_{0}}+O((\tau+M)^{-\beta_{0}-\nu})

for some
\nu>0
and therefore

\int_{0}^{\infty}(T^{-1}\phi)^{2}|_{r=r_{0}}\,d\tau\gtrsim\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}[\phi]\int_{0}^{\infty}(\tau+M)^{-2\beta_{0}}\,d\tau=\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{when $\beta_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}$},

which is a contradiction. The above failure of (
4.5
) to hold is related to the fact that the
V_{\alpha}
-term in (
3.1
) cannot be dealt with by simply treating it as an inhomogeneity in the derivation of (
4.5
) for
V_{\alpha}\equiv 0
.

We avoid the contradiction above by adding growing
r
-weights to the right-hand side of (
4.5
):
when
\alpha<0
(or equivalently,
\beta_{0}<1
), we prove in particular the inequality:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}\phi^{2}+r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})[\phi]\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{0}}r^{p}(X(r\phi))^{2}+r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[\phi]+r^{2}\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{n}_{0})[T\phi]\,d\sigma dr,

(4.7)

with
p>1-\beta_{0}
. One may easily verify that the right-hand side of (
4.7
) blows up for the time integral
T^{-1}\phi
that was introduced in the contradiction argument above, so the numerology of the decay rates in the late-time tails for
T^{-1}\phi
that follows Theorem
4.1
does not lead to a contradiction with (
4.7
). For the decay mechanism in
Step 2
below, it will in fact be sufficient to apply the above estimate with
p=1
.

•

A robust physical space method for deriving integrated energy decay

To prove (
4.7
), we use a new argument that is sufficiently robust so as to apply to the general class of potentials
V_{\alpha}
. This argument provides moreover an alternative to existing methods of deriving (
4.5
) when
V_{\alpha}\equiv 0
. We decompose
\phi
into spherical harmonic modes
\phi_{\ell}
and consider two cases:

A)

\alpha\leq 0
and
\ell=0
,

B)

\alpha>0
or
\ell\geq 1
.

We obtain an integrated energy estimate in the case
A)
by considering the renormalized quantity

\check{\phi}_{0}=w_{0}^{-1}\phi_{0},

which as shown in Lemma
3.4
satisfies a wave equation
with no zeroth order terms
.

In view of the late-time asymptotics of Theorem
4.1
, this quantity will have the property that its derivatives, both in the
T
and
X
directions, will decay
faster
than
\check{\phi}_{0}
itself. This is reflected in the fact that we are able to obtain the following integrated energy estimate:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}(T\check{\phi}_{0})^{2}+(X\check{\phi}_{0})^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{0}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(N,\mathbf{n}_{0})[\phi]\,d\sigma dr,

(4.8)

which, in contrast with (
4.7
), has
no
extra term with growing
r
-weights on the right-hand side. We obtain (
4.8
) by multiplying both sides of (
3.9
) with the vector field multiplier
r^{-q}Z\check{\phi}_{0}
, where
q>0
is taken suitably large. The estimate (
4.8
) can then be applied in combination with
r
-weighted energy estimates (see Step 2 below) to conclude (
4.7
).

In case
B)
, we further split the spherical harmonics as follows:

(i)

\ell\leq\ell_{0}
,

(ii)

\ell>\ell_{0}
,

with
\ell_{0}
sufficiently large. In case
(ii)
, the angular derivatives dominate the terms involving
V_{\alpha}
by the Poincaré inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, so standard Morawetz estimates can be used with a straightforward vector field multiplier: we multiply both sides of (
3.4
) with
f(r)Z\psi+\frac{D}{2}\frac{df}{dr}\psi
, where
f(r)=-r^{-1}(r-3M)
and integrate by parts. It is only in this case that the presence of trapped null geodesics at
r=3M
plays a role.

In case
(i)
, we consider again the renormalized quantities

\check{\phi}_{\ell}=w_{\ell}^{-1}\phi_{\ell}.

As in case
A)
above, these are quantities that will have both
T
and
X
derivatives that decay
faster
in time than the quantity itself. In particular, we can show that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}}(T\check{\phi}_{\ell})^{2}+(X\check{\phi}_{\ell})^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\leq C_{\ell}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}r^{2}\mathbb{T}(T,\mathbf{n}_{0})[\phi_{\ell}]\,d\sigma dr

(4.9)

by multiplying both sides of (
3.9
) with the vector field multiplier
r^{-q}Z\check{\phi}_{\ell}
, with
q>0
sufficiently large.

Finally, we combine (
4.9
) with standard Morawetz/integrated energy decay estimates involving straightforward multipliers in a region
r\geq R
, where
R>2M
is sufficiently large: that is to say, we simply multiply (
3.4
) with
-Z\psi
and integrate by parts in
\{r\geq R\}
, using that the boundary terms along
r=R
are already controlled via (
4.9
).

We note that in all cases, the consideration of the renormalized quantities
\check{\phi}_{\ell}
allows us to circumvent the need for delicately chosen vector field multipliers. So even in the
V_{\alpha}\equiv 0
setting, this approach provides a cleaner alternative to previous proofs of integrated energy decay estimates on spherically symmetric black hole spacetimes.

Step 1
is carried out in detail in §
6
.

Step 2:
A decay mechanism for time derivatives via a hierarchy of
r
-weighted energy estimates

Equipped with an integrated energy estimate (restricted to
r\leq R
), we will obtain energy decay in time by considering a hierarchy of
r
-weighted energy estimates in the region
r\geq R
, for suitably large
R
. These take the schematic form:

\begin{split}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{2}}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p}(L\psi)^{2}\,d\sigma dr+\>&\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p-1}(L\psi)^{2}+(2-p)r^{p-3}(\phi^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi|^{2})\,d\sigma dr\right]d\tau\\
\lesssim&\>\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p}(L\psi)^{2}\,d\sigma dr+\ldots.\end{split}

(4.10)

In the case
\alpha\geq 0
, (
4.10
) holds for
0<p\leq 2
and it first appeared in
[
DR10
]
. These
r
-weighted energy estimates can be translated into energy decay in time via the following principle: restrict to
p\geq 1
, then

\textbf{energy decay rate in time}\>=\>\mathbf{1}+\textbf{range of $p$}.

For example, if
p\in[1,2]
, then the energy decay rate will be 2. This principle follows from an application of the mean-value theorem along an appropriate dyadic sequence of times (“the pigeonhole principle”).

When
\alpha<0
, however, the
V_{\alpha}
-term contributes to leading-order with a bad sign and, after applying a Hardy inequality, (
4.10
) is only valid for the smaller range
1-\beta_{0}<p<1+\beta_{0}
. As
\beta_{0}\downarrow 0
, the range of allowed
p
therefore decreases to 0, so according to the principle above we are only above to prove energy decay with a rate smaller than
1+\beta_{0}\downarrow 1
. To obtain the desired sharp decay in time,
it will turn out to be sufficient to consider just (
4.10
) with
p=1
, provided we also commute with
T
,
rL
and angular derivatives.

Indeed, we apply (
3.4
) together with
T=L+\underline{L}
to relate the
T
-derivative to the remaining derivatives as follows: for
r>R

r^{p+1}(LT\psi)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 1}r^{p-1}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}L(rL)^{n_{2}}\psi|^{2}+r^{p-1}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}+1}(rL)^{n_{2}}\psi|^{2}.

Via the above, we can in fact control

\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p+1}(LT\psi)^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau,

using that (
4.10
) also holds under commutation with angular derivatives and
rL
.
We therefore effectively gain of two powers in
r
, at the expense of replacing
\psi
by
T\psi
, which translates into two extra powers in the energy decay rate in time according to the principle above.
The existence of longer
r
-weighted hierarchies for
T\psi
after commutation with
rL
were first exploited in
[
Sch13
,
Mos16
]
and played an important role in
[
AAG18b
,
AAG21a
,
AAG21b
]
. In the present paper, we additionally need to apply an interpolation argument to compensate for the fact that for every commutation with
T
we only gain
one
extra estimate in the
r
-weighted hierarchy, but we want to gain
two
powers in the corresponding energy decay rate. We derive energy decay with the rate
\tau^{-3}
for
T\phi
and, by repeating this strategy, energy decay of
T^{N}\phi
with the rate
\tau^{-1-2N}
, with on the right-hand side energy norms involving in particular higher-order
rL
-derivatives of
\psi
itself.

Step 2
is carried out in detail in §§
7
–
8
.

Step 3:
Sharp decay via time integrals and elliptic estimates

While
Step 2
allows us to obtain arbitrarily high polynomial decay rates in time (given sufficiently regular initial data), we are only able to make such statements for
T^{N}\phi
with appropriately high
N
and not for the original solution
\phi
. To remedy this, we will act (formally) with the operator
T^{-N}
. In other words, we consider the time integrals:

\begin{split}T^{-N}\phi(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=&(-1)^{N}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{\tau_{N-1}}^{\infty}\phi(s,r,\theta,\varphi)\,dsd\tau_{N-1}\ldots d\tau_{1}\\
=&\>-\frac{1}{(N-1)!}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}(\tau-s)^{N-1}\phi(s,r,\theta,\varphi)\,ds,\end{split}

which appear also in Theorem
4.1
. The objects
T^{-N}\phi
are well-defined if
\phi
decays suitably fast in time. Furthermore,
T(T^{-N}\phi)=T^{-N+1}\phi
and, by the fact that
\square_{g_{M}}
commutes with
T
(due to the stationarity property of the Schwarzschild metric),
T^{-N}\phi
are (formally) solutions to (
3.1
) if
\phi
is a solution.
11
11

11

Note that in the setting of time-dependent or nonlinear wave equations, where one cannot appeal to stationarity, the definition of
T^{-N}\phi
still makes sense, provided
\phi
decays suitably fast in time. However, in this case,
T^{-N}\phi
will not satisfy the same wave equation as
\phi
; it will instead satisfy a modified wave equation.

Rather than working directly with the above definition of
T^{-N}\phi
, however, which requires
a priori
sufficient time decay in the definition, we instead determine the initial data
(T^{-N}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}},T(T^{-N}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}})
that evolve in time to form the solution
T^{-N}\phi
. We consider first the case
N=1
. Then
T(T^{-1}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}}=\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
, so it remains only to find
T^{-1}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
. We find
T^{-1}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
by rearranging (
3.1
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\phi=

\displaystyle\>F[T\phi],\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\cdot)=

\displaystyle\>X(Dr^{2}X(\cdot))+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)-r^{2}V_{\alpha}(\cdot),

\displaystyle F[(\cdot)]=

\displaystyle\>2r(1-h)X(r(\cdot))-\frac{dh}{dr}r^{2}(\cdot)+r^{2}h\tilde{h}T(\cdot).

We therefore construct
T^{-1}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
by solving the equation
\mathcal{L}(T^{-1}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}})=F[\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}]
, where the right-hand side is known, as it is determined by the initial data for
\phi
. This is a degenerate elliptic equation (with a degeneracy at the horizon). We obtain a solution by constructing
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
on appropriate energy spaces. Given sufficiently rapidly decaying initial data we can repeat this procedure to obtain
T^{-n}\phi
, for
n\leq N
, where the exponent
N
is determined by the requirement that
T^{-N}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
still has finite
r
-weighted energies with appropriate powers in
r
so as to be able to apply Step 2.
12
12

12

Strictly speaking, we will encounter the situation where
T^{-N}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
has an infinite
p=1

r
-weighted energy. To obtain an sharp energy decay rate, we will then need to approach
T^{-N}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
with a sequence of compactly supported functions and apply an interpolation argument to convert the extra growth in
r
of the initial data to extra growth in
\tau
compared to the case of a finite
p=1
energy.
It turns out that we will need to take
N=n_{\beta_{0}}+1
here. For
\beta_{0}<1
, we therefore take
N=1
.

We see that
the total number of
r
-weighted estimates in the hierarchy is determined by the
r
-asymptotics of
T^{-n_{\beta_{0}}-1}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
.
In other words, the
r
-asymptotics of sufficiently many time integrals form the bottleneck for extending further the hierarchy of
r
-weighted estimates from
Step 2
and obtaining faster decay in time. These
r
-asymptotics in turn reflect conformal regularity properties at null infinity. Even if the initial data for
\phi
are smooth and compactly supported, the initial data for its time integrals will generically have finite polynomial decay in
r
as
r\to\infty
, which then determines the decay rates appearing in the late-time asymptotics. Starting with initial data that decay slower in
r
, simply means that fewer time integrals might be needed to form a bottleneck.

In summary, with the above method involving
r
-weighted energy estimates for time derivatives, we are able to make the following principle
quantitative
:

\textbf{faster $r$-decay of (multiple) time integrals at infinity}\implies\textbf{faster $\tau$-decay}.

(4.11)

Step 3
is carried out in §
9
.

From
Step 2
and
Step 3
it is already possible to obtain
almost-sharp decay in time
i.e. uniform decay-in-time estimates with a rate that is
\epsilon
smaller than the sharp rate, with
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small. However, this is not the approach taken in the present paper, where we instead establish sharp decay-in-time estimates directly and moreover identify the leading-order term in the late-time asymptotics
at the same time
; see Step 4 below.

The (
r
-weighted) energy decay can be used to obtain pointwise decay for
r\phi
and
\sqrt{r}\phi
. To obtain faster decay for
\phi
and
r^{-q}\phi
, with appropriate
q>0
, i.e. to exchange powers of
r
for powers of
\tau^{-1}
, we prove faster decay for energies with negative
r
-weights by applying elliptic estimates for the equation
\mathcal{L}[\phi]=F[T\phi]
that also played a role in constructing time integral data in
Step 3
. See also
[
AAG21a
,
AAG21b
]
for details on the use of elliptic estimates to control energies with negative
r
-weights for wave equations without a potential.

Step 4:
Exact leading-order asymptotics and late-time tails via subtraction

Rather than deriving the almost-sharp decay estimates of
\phi
, we directly derive sharp decay, and in fact, we directly derive the precise leading-order asymptotics in
\tau^{-1}
by proving uniform decay-in-time estimates for the difference function:

\widehat{\phi}=\phi-\Phi^{(\beta_{0})},

where
\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}(\tau,r)
corresponds to the leading-order late-time behaviour of
\phi
that we want to prove. We will show that
\widehat{\phi}
decays
faster
than
\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}
, which implies that the function
\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}
indeed determines completely the leading-order late-time behaviour.

More precisely, we take

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}(\tau,r)=\Phi_{0}w_{0}(r)(\tau+1+2r)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}(\tau+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}},

with
\Phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}(\tau,r)
has the above form, then the expression
(\square_{g_{M}}-V_{\alpha})\widehat{\phi}
, while not zero, will decay sufficiently fast in
\tau
and
r
, so that the methods of
Step 2
still apply even with
\phi
replaced by
\widehat{\phi}
. The nice decay properties of
(\square_{g_{M}}-V_{\alpha})\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}
are related to the fact that when
M=0
,
\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}
is a (spherically symmetric) solution to

(\square-\alpha r^{-2})\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}=0.

with
\square
the standard wave operator on Minkowski spacetime. This observation also plays an important role in
[
GVdM22
]
.

In order to show that
\widehat{\phi}
decays faster than the expected sharp behaviour, we need to fix an appropriate choice for the constant
\Phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. It turns out that by fixing

\Phi_{0}=\frac{(-1)^{n_{\beta_{0}}}n_{\beta_{0}}!}{2^{n_{\beta_{0}}-\beta_{0}}\pi}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\beta_{0}+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\beta_{0}-1}{2}-n_{\beta_{0}}\right)}{\Gamma(\beta_{0}-n_{\beta_{0}})}\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}[\phi]

and considering the time integral
T^{-1-n_{\beta_{0}}}\Phi^{(\beta_{0})}
, we obtain that

X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{0}}}\Phi^{(\beta_{0})})|_{\Sigma_{0}}\sim\mathfrak{I}_{\beta_{0}}[\phi]r^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}+n_{\beta_{0}}}\hskip 10.00002pt(r\to\infty)

which is the same asymptotic behaviour as that of
X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{0}}}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}}
(see
(iv)
of Theorem
4.1
). Therefore, the difference
X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{0}}}\widehat{\phi})|_{\Sigma_{0}}

must decay faster in
r
than
X(rT^{-1-n_{\beta_{0}}}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{0}}
and we are able to, via
Step 2
and
Step 3
, get more decay in
\tau
for
\widehat{\phi}
compared to the sharp decay of
\phi
, adhering to the principle (
4.11
).

Step 4
is carried out in §
10
.

Step 5:
Refined estimates for higher-order spherical modes

In order to obtain the leading-order late-time behaviour of
\phi_{\geq\ell}
, with faster decay in
\tau
, we note the following rough correspondence:

\square_{g_{M}}\phi_{\geq\ell}=V_{\alpha}\phi_{\geq\ell}\hskip 10.00002pt\longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\square_{g_{M}}\phi_{\geq 0}=V_{\alpha+\ell(\ell+1)}\phi_{\geq 0}.

We obtain this by simply rearranging:

r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}-V_{\alpha}=r^{-2}(\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+\ell(\ell+1))-(V_{\alpha}+\ell(\ell+1)r^{-2})

and noting that the angular operator
-(\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+\ell(\ell+1))
has non-negative eigenvalues when restricted to functions projected on spherical harmonics with degree
\geq\ell
